PRACTICAL POULTRY FARMING.

(By Redcap.)

PRESERVING EGGS.

- Nov tlird the,. isa greater supply of eggs c ming in, it would be a good p'aa to preserve a few batches I an. not an advocate of prmerved eggs for market, because you canna always rely upon their soundness, but at the same time they are useful for culinary purposes. When so used it helps you to have a better supply for winter markrts; therefore in that sense it it advisable to preserve a few lots. There ate two methods of preserving eggs, the water-glass system and the other primes is lime. M ATER-1

To one pound of water-glass, add about six quarts of lsot water. After yim have well stirred this, leave to cool off, them pour the liquid into an earthenware vowel, and place the latter in a col place. The eggs may be put in daily as you collect them. Continue to add to until the vessel is nearly full, but at least a couple of inches should remain above the eggs. The water-glass can be procured from a:most any chemist. When putting the eggs away oee that none are cracked and do act tut thin-shel!ed ones in tither.

Pour one gallon of water on to 11b. of quicklime. The day following well atir the mixture and add to it 6 or. of salt and I oz. of cream of tatter, nnd mix the whole ingredent t well together. When the sediment has settled place the eggs in an earthenware vessel and pour over them the clear liquid. You may try both these processes of preserving eggs and adopt in the future the most reliable. Let me remind you that when a preserved egg is to be boil. ii, that the large end should first of all be pierced with a Mo. This allows the gases to ware and der idedly adds to the freshness of flavour when boiled.

MARKETING EGG,

When the supply of any commrdity is great it is quality which commands a ready sale. Therefore you should of necessity understand something of the art of marketing eggs. First of alit should separate the white ones front the brown rms. Then I should g, over them again and take rut all the smallest eggs— these le- the way could he treed for preserving. Then I should wash all tainted ones ha, re finally packing. Now some people mix the eggs, but I prefer to have them separate, you could have one basket of brown ones undone with white eggs. They show to greater advantage and at the wi-h of customers they could be mised when purchased. Now at the present time we are getting a great supply of egga and as already stared it is the quality which will Fell the eggs. It will doubly repay you for the trouble you may spend in sorting, wishing, and grading. Another important point hile dealing with the egg question is flavour. It does not always prove that a nice white-ehelled egg spells fine flavour. And unless the laying stock have access loan unlimited supply of clean drinking water, and ale kept away from kul middens and the like, you cannot hope to have eggs of a good flavour. Everything depends on dens wholesome food and pure water ; given the=e you need have no doules. All the foregoing remarks are important to those of my readers who make a epeciality of fresh eggs for market purpoees.You grill not only please your customer n by so doing but add new ones to your list. blight I to, pardoned fern bit of personal pride ? When by chance afr end may dine with me and an egg forms a part of the meal the flavour is alwaye commemed upon. I simply make the reference to prove that it is because I feed on good food, and supply dean water in abundance that makes the flavour of the eggs no appetising.

THE MONTH OF M It.

This month should I e shwa the busiest of all, especially with those who cater for a seaside trade. To such, let me say, that no time should be loet. Hatch every available egg and rear every chick possible, bruit. oath. reason progresses the demand will increase. In fact, I look forward to a record in both the demand and consumption of British products. This is the month also when spring-cleaning ought to be done. All houses, coops and nest boxes should be well lime-washed and the exist toe of the hbuses and runs tarred. You should select a day and windy day tor lime-washing, as it in detrimental to the stcck if they go to roost in damp houses. It will not be out of place ill rewind you of a suitable preparation. Get a bucket and place in it a piece of quick lime about the sine of one head, pear over it boiling water, to which has been added a pint of paraffin oil. The latter lea good insecticide, and when so used it ins veritable insect destroyer. It is also a good plan to apt inkle a few handfuls of rpricklitire on the Our of the house and work it into every niche and crevice. Every perch should be removed and the perch-sockets well soaked with paraffin, for it in here where the red mite makes its home.

I do not suppose for a moment that you can net every hen that becomes broody, thelefote you should adopt some method of inducing tbmn kr return to mire profitable operations. The old-fashioned and inhuman methods of ducking them overhead in water and starving them for A few days need not he !everted to. All that you um d is a spare coop wherein to place the broodies. Let the mop be no that they cannot perch anywhere but on the floor of the coop Do not starve them but rather on the other hand fet d them liberally on plenty of wholesome soft food and lean meat. This will excite the actions of the ovary and they will soon come on to lay again. Let the coop be no placed that they may constantly see the met of the at, ck. Quiet- nese will only Add to broody propensity while trcite- meat helps thr m to break it off.

The changeableness of -ibs. weather and no much cold rain brings with it quite an epidemic of colds in the p( ultry yard. I have had to treat a good number of late which have been suffering with severe colds. I have only one remedy to offer p u, it is timple. but very effective. Take a bruin of strong Condis Fluid, bold the affected one by the legs and with the rigl.t band tale bold of the wattles 'and dip its bead right into the fluid. Continue to do this twice a day for several days and keep in a warm place and they will soma be alright again As sOOO as you detect any

HORSE DEALERS CLAIM.

Six Years Old Case Lost.

At the —Warrington County Court on Thursday, his Honour Judge H.Y.Stanger, K.C.,delivered jerboas nt In the action which ass held at the previous Fitting of the Court, in which Mr. Enoch William Sankey, horse dealer, Croft, sued Mesas. Walker and lloniftaye, brewers, for £6B for • bay mare which plaintiff alleged be had sold defendants in March of 1905. Mr. Harold Smith, M.P. (inatructed by Nlemes. R. Davies and CO, appeared for the plaintiff and Mr. Wingate Saul (instructed by Mem. Sutton and Co., blanchester), for the defendant. In giving judgment his Honour asked what the con- tract was that was mede in March 1903? The plaintiff mid thst the contract wan that the horse would co into the defendants' possession after two days trial and after it had neared a veterinary surgeon. On the other band the defendant said the contract was that they stern to try the horse for 23 day. and that they were not to be responaible for an, damage that might le done to tbe horse during that time. The plaintiff had co one to rely on except him- self, and he had no memoranda to corroborate his view of the ttansaction, except that he said that If such term. which the defendants alleged to have been made, had been mentioned he would not have parted with the horse. It appeared from the cross-examination that there was at least one occasion on which the Man- chester Corporation bad purchased a hoise from the p!aintiff on such terms. For the defendant, Mr. Allto femployed by Jlesars. Walker and Homfrayei and Mr. Robinson mid that the contract was for 28 days trial, and this was also shove by • copy of a letter which tly y said they bad sent to the plaintiff to receipt .rf the horse. Mr. Harold Smith, who eppeered fur the plaiatiff end who put the asee with groat force and great ability, said that if the letter had bran received it would have confirmed defendants' case. Them was also sufficient evidence to show that whin the horse died before the end of the month the aelendents wrote to the plaintiff repudiating liability and later forwarded El,the money obtained by the sale of the onrcese. On the shot. looking .oth. correspondence in conjunction with the verbal testimony he thought the weight of saltier sew.. plainly on the aide of the defendant,. Ili. Honour Love judgeteot for the delve:lmmo.

IRISH AFFAIRS. NOTES FOR IRISH READERS.

===== 0002601_19110506_art0011.txt | included=true | included =====
VETO BILL'S PROGRESS.

Wednesday night of this week marked an event of more than passing interest in the ; history of modern politics. The Veto Bill , finished its long and arduous Committee stage, the preamble being dealt with at a , single sitting. It is true that the meaning and purport of the preamble—that is the reform of the House of Lords—had been debated over and over again, but still the celerity with which the later clauses of the Bill have been disposed of compares remarkably with the absurd prolonging of the talking on the trivial point at the outset. Never perhaps have we seen an opposition go to pieces as the decomposi- tion in the Unionist ranks shewn in the past few days. The collapse of their obstruction was of such suddenness as to cause some people to suspect that an arrangement had been arrived at between the Leaders of the Government and the Opposition, but on the Ministerial side no countenance whatever is given to this suggestion. The Budget being down for May Ist. means that the Veto Bill will leave the House of Commons after the second and third read- ings early next week. There is a strong impression now growing up that the Peers will deal with the Veto Bill without undue delay, since they have nothing to gain by prolonging the procedure. It is expected by those who are qualified to know that in the committee stage,which will be as brief in the ' Lords as it has been protracted in the Commons, the Peers will probably confine ! their amendments to one—the exclusion of Home Rule from the operation of the Bill. The core of the political importance of , the hour will again be proved to be Self Government for Ireland—and we Irish are ; quite ready for the fight. A NOTABLE HAND-SHAKE.

The presentation recently made to the Speaker and Mrs. Lowther, in celebration of their silver wedding, was quite a unique compliment. But there was a significance in the ceremony not noticed in the Press, which will surely appeal to Irish and English alike. From the time the Irish members entered Parliament down to to-day, they have always been in the position of a permanent minority. The Nationalist members have, for o.ivious reasons stood aloof horn the social ' life of the House, as a legislative assembly, and have consistently remained outside. What finer token could be found then of the better conditions existing between the two peoples than to find Mr. Redmond participating in a tinction which no Leader of the Irish party could possibly have attended fifteen or twenty years ago? Two incidents marked the gathering which shewed that the spirit of conciliation and goodwill was abroad. Mr. Balfour and Mr. Macdonald ascended the plat- form together—Conservative and Labour— and when Mr. Redmond and Mr. Balfour met they shook hands. We have it from an authentic source that the last occasion on which they shook hands was when Mr. Redmond met Mr. Balfour after the Irish leader's release from jail twenty years ago. A GROWING INDUSTRY.

Apropos of Irish commercial possibilities to which we have called attention here recently it is at once an interesting and remarkable fact that an Irish made dis- temper was specified for the huge triumphal arches erected in Cape Town in connection with the opening of the South African Parliament. Messrs. D. Anderson and Son, feimited, of Belfast, whose tender was , accepted in open competition, are to be heartily congratulated on this interesting success of a growing Irish industry. This firm's products have also been used, we understand, with very satistactory results in all the Government buildings and offices. We are delighted to learn that the advantage of this form of decoration, together with the beautiful range of tints and colours in which it is made by this progressing firm, assure for it a permanent and an increasing market.

KNOWLEDGE AND PROSPERITY. The case just cited is typical of the general prosperity which trade returns for Ireland are shewing. That the country is undoubt- edly growing prosperous may be seen in the comparative statement of its trade to-day and that of six years ago. In international trading in that short periect there has been an increase of more than twenty millions, Whilst there are no statistics to shew to what extent the acreage under tillage has increased or decreased, which information by the way is of vital importance, it is quite unmistakeable that Irish imports and exports have been steadily increasing every year since 1904. Of the chief contributory causes to this development, we are inclined to put land legislation first, and we imagine few people who know anything of the

agrarian conditions of the past in Ireland will dissent from that view. Another important influence and one which is not quite as well known, but to which some of the recent development is due, is the work of the Department of Agriculture and Technical Instruction. In such phases of its work as the live stock scheme for improving the quality of cattle, in its up-to-date scientific agriculture propaganda, poultry rearing and domestic economy,the department has quietly but effectively done work which has gone to the roots in improving the condition of life in Ireland. But perhaps its most permanent work is to be found in its provision for Technical Instruction. Industrial development of the kind we have in Ireland depends almost entirely on the efficiency of its workers. Trained Lancashire cotton operatives with nearly a century of tradition of craft in their fingers, would for a long time lead in any race for output. In this industrial training, which of course does not pretend to the almost automatic skill of Lancashire, the saools, lecturers and experts of the Department must have materially helped Ireland. We see from its last annual report that there were upwards of forty. do thousand students attending classes in the schools under its con- trol. It is one of the most encouraging signs of the thne that the most serious and strong- est force in Ir'sh education during the past ten years has been directed towards the , improvement of technical methods, which must have not only great effect on the trade and home industries, but also have influenced the temper of Irish thought and turned the thoughts of the younger generation to indus- trial enterprises and to the means of gaining efficiency in them. In an earlier time, the poet was the mightiest man in Erin, and kings shook with fear on their thrones at his reproach. We foresee that in the Ireland of to-morrow the scientist may occupy the place of the poet in the far away yesterday. As in all parts of the world where minds are broadening and intellects quickening, the reign of the men who know, the reign of the scientists is beginning. And without bend- ing the knee to materialism, we cannot help thinking that whilst they will rule over a more prosperous people, we do not see why they should not create as fine if not finer souls as we associate with the power of the older poets.

===== 0002601_19110506_art0012.txt | included=true | included =====

GOLF.

" MACBETH."

The " Law" and the Prophets. Al Approclation.—By Alfred Perris There is a good Italian proverb —" Tutto iivervollo non in una terns ” All the brains are not to one head. Eve!), man is bound to do, whatever it may lie in inn to do, with all his might, and tine firm centre to MY own small circle of experience and thought. But ho is not called upon to think himself the axis o. the Coemos. Ile known nothing, and is able to leara nothing, who has not learnt that upon may points lie may he wrong. The strongest individual convivion ought not to Lo inconaiat .nt with the power of applying to oneself tl a law that is applicable o all other men. We tee men every day whom we think none the less wrong because they are positive, and eve ought to admit, , ' each for himself, the difference between a personal conviction and an abstract truth. The writer trusts, therefore, that Ids readers will allow, throughout, for the underlying rtservation of respect to diffei ing opinions, even in pnasages in which he may give firmest expresaion of bin own belief. He has on many occasions, been at variance with opinions expreased by critics for whom be has held the highest respect. Intellectual men have accredited actors with poetic , insight which he has failed to recognise. It is well I that there should be such differences of opinion, ' without them there tv,uld be no constant shaking to and fro of the great seine of argument, no exercising I of man's intellect _

Suro, he that made us with such largo discourse li,oking before and after, gave us not That cap itility and gnd-like reason, To lust in us, unus'd." . .

The play begins by throwing the pall of witchcraft, and the superstition which goes with it, together with all their baneful ielluences, over the leading personage of the drama. The neird sisters are ationialh a, of whom we know not whence they are eprung, or whether they 11,1,0 beginning or ending. 'I hey ere without human passions and seers to be without human relation. Mr. Law, in his presentaton of these figures (for to him most he ascribed the credit of the conception and realis dim of the really awesome character of the mysterious rites precti-ed by these creatures), has shown a most thoughtful and intelligent appreciation of the purpose of the dramatist, and deserves the highest praise for the evidently indefatigable and persident pains he has taken in coaching the members of the company who played the witches, to ensure thet the rigidly serious and deadly earnest nature of their incantations and observances should be maintained without • breach through all their appearances, and with the aid of Messrs. WARRINGTON FOOTBALL CLUB. CRITICAL TIMES AHEAD Review of Season 191011.

By "enlist.'

The close of the 1910-11 footbal treason finds the Warrington Club faced skit one or the nwst critical periods in its history, and very much teal depend on the enterprise mil acumen of the Committee and officials during the ensuing twelve month.. The crisis Iran not arisen suddenly ; it has been coming for two yeara, but no one with " half an tye "can deny that it has now reached en acute stage. A year ago when penning this annuli review I drew attention to the fact that such men as Tilley, Dickenson, Taylor, Fish, Brook., Jenkinv, Boardman, Belton and O'N, ill were pint their best and a ould SIC Ito be gradually replaced by younger and ape, dice players if the quality of the Ciub'n football was going t.c be main- tained-. All lut three of thess men have done duty again almost continn only throughout the treason just closed, and with the inevitable result. In the League Warrington have dropped term sixth to ninth place, with an average if 56.45 against 61.76 h year ago. But the most significant feature of the League campaign from a Wiwi, loon point of view is the rletsrioration in play as et ideoced by the aggregate of points "for " and " against." 1-m.t reason War- rington termed 408 p its against 252 ; this sea. .n 281 against 303. For the first thee in right newtons the balance icon the wrong side. Could anything be more conclusive of the fact that as a team Warrington have been &Ina steadily down, and that now they have touched what should be rock bottom ? Ti e same plums have been the backbone of the team during the whole of these eight seasons. Can they be depended op n for am ther term? I say emphatically no and 1 think tte whole of the supporters of the Club trill agree with me. Fish has retired —and the measure of his loss was immediately epparent. lle wan the "one And only," and the team has been poorer in both the playing and the epectacnlar sense eter since. Such a place as he filled in the eyes of the football public, and in the scoring records of the Warrington Club, made the loss quite irreparable in Ike el are of one seagon. lint quite apart from the retirement of the Warrington "star" there is the awkward fact that all fir ee players mentioned above ore one year older. If they were past their best last /warm, they can only be eon- widered as veterans to-day, nnd while a veteran tan his value to any team a combination of veterens can have no chance of competing suceensfully with youth, skill, and enthusiasm. Pondbly I am uttering trui,ms, but the fact has ti be hammered h me that the Warrionton Club has readied the rage when it can no longer rely on those aforesaid veterans to keep it in the first ffinht of Northern CM n Clubs , Referring again t my remarks of a year ago-1 ' pouted to Broughton Rangers as n club which hod just pasted dirt ugh the sauce viciesitudisand was engaged in the big lank of building up a new team. What is the result to-day? The Rangers have a young,improvirg ream and they have won the Cap! In this fact lies the germ of hope and optimii,m for Warrington. They are placed in difficulties, lott they are not yet in the Slough of Despond. What Broughton have done with a less soli 1 financi4 1.42. Werringtoe may do if the reins of management are handled with a sure and firm hand. IVe require rt nein team, and the sooner the task if equipping dm club with the requisite leavening of youngerocapable members and the better. As a club we mainot afford to wait. An expenditure of over 11,200 has just been embarked upon in connection with the ground, and the experience of die pant season has shown that unless a winning team can be got together theca is going to be a serious loss of revenue in the near future. Football enditisiaetn are fickle folk, and unless their appetite can lie stimulated by the tight kind of fare, they 1,11 go e'sewlit re f,,,p,rt and enjoyment. Personally, I have little doubt that the Committee and c fficials of the Club tralise the exigencies of the eiruation on clearly as anyone else, and I have confidence in their determination to leave no atone untuinzd to taise the Club to

its former statue But we have a general meeting approaching, and there may be change, in the governing body. I hear of one or two possib'e vacancies on the Committee, and it is in fi ling these that I Wi.h to give counsel to the members. They , must realise their individual responsibility and FOO FO it that any new men who are appointed possess, buriners ability as well as a knowledge of football. The Warrington Club is now a big concern, feed with all the competition that is found in the strpgale to earn a living, and to be successfully managed it must be in the hands of nnen with real ability and strength of character. I hope that men if the right stamp will come forward and secure the support mcesEary to place them al,ngside the faithful band of men who have for so long maintained Warrington football at a high level. A year ago we had to be content with the West Lancashire Cup, and the "A " team Championship. This year, even these small trophies have gone elsewhere. Miscellaneous.

The PRIME MINISTER THE NEW explained to the House SECOND of Commons on Wednes- CHAMBER. day why it will become

necessary to proceed at some time during the life of the present Parliament with the Preamble of the Parlia- ment Bill. The Preamble declares the expediency of making provisions for regulat- ing the relations between the two Houses of Parliament, and the intention of substitut- ing for the House of Lords as it at present exists a second chamber constituted on a popular instead of a hereditary basis. There are many wise and able men in the country who do not admit the necessity for any second chamber. There are others who do not greatly care what kind of second chamber there may be when the operative clauses of the Parliament Bill have been passed into law. The first of these points of view was set forth very clearly by Mr. BARNES on behalf of the Labour party, and it proved, as in fact it was certain to prove. , not to be the point of view of the House of I Commons. The contention that the House of Lords may as well be left alone if its absolute I veto be abolished was answered by Mr. ASQUITH in terms which, as it must have been felt, threw an unexpected light on the whole subject. The Parliament Bill, the PRIME MINISTER pointed out, is only a partial solution of the constitutional question and it is desirable to guard Democracy against a future collusion between the upper house and a Conservative House of Commons by reconstructing the second chamber. As a reason for reform, this explanation seems unanswerable.

The position of the Labour Party was stated very moderately by Mr. G. N. BARNES. In the opinion of the member for the Blackfriars Division of Glasgow and his colleagues, a second chamber is an unnecessary and useless encumbrance. The PRIME MINISTER admitted that he was at one time himself of that opinion ; hut time and experience, he said, have modi- fied the view which be held in his more impulsive period, and he is now satisfied that in the interestKof the country it is desirable that there should be a properly-consti- tuted second chamber, clothed with defined and limited functions, and acting as the organ of the popular will—not pre- dominantly partisan in its composition, not excessive in numbers, a body capable of exeicising the functions of revision, consulta- tion, and delay. That is a really admirable definition of the only sort of second chamber that will henceforth be regarded as tolerable' The PRIME MINISTER'S language on the subject quite excludes the proposals that, so far as is known, will be embodied in the MARQUIS OF LANSDOWNE' Bill, which will be based on the retention of the hereditary principle as regards the main portion of the reformed House. tinder the Government's proposals it is clear that the hereditary principle as it affects legislation will be swept away once for all.

It is amazing to find how much even the leading men of the Opposition have changed their tone in respect to the present Constitu- tion of the upper house. Who would have expected, only a few months since, to hear Mr. BALFOUR saying what he said on Wednesday? The present purely hereditary second chamber, he observed, is not strong enough to perform second chamber functions. By " strong," he meant something else, of course. The House of Lords has proved itself strong enough, and a great deal too

strong. It is not fair enough ; that is the real trouble. The exercise of its powers under present conditions, the leader of the Opposition is at length convinced, is some- thing that can no longer be defended. He even gives up the contention that the House of Lords should exercise co-ordinate power with the House of Commons, A second chamber, he contends, should not wield equal authority with the popular chamber. This is said because he does not wish the new second chamber to be wholly representative; but his words mean surrender along the whole line. The Government may already count upon a victory achieved. There will still be many feints and ambushes and sham battles, no doubt; but the Parliament Bill will pass into law without the necessity of a resort to the ultimately means of persuasion. So much now seems clear.

The local arrangements THE CORONATION for the Coronation AND festivities have again WALKING DAY, been in the melting pot this week and an Important change in the programme is announced to-day. One of the main objects of the committee has been to impress the young idea with the importance of the occasion by organising a big field day for the youngsters in Victoria Park on Corona- tion Day, as a prelude to the usual Walking Day procession and sports on the following day. Many people have all along failed to see how the arrangement was to work satisfactorily, for there is a limit even to juvenile endurance—to say nothing of the trials of the adult workers who would have beet called upon to share

_ the hard work on both occasions—and -some went so far as to advocate the extreme course of abandoning the Walking Day pro- cession. Now, however, all difficulties are removed by the decision of the Coronation Committee not to hold the proposed children's treat in Victoria Park on Coronation Day, and the Sunday school teachers and officials will thus be able to concentrate all their attention on making the Walking Day of 1911 a conspicuously memorable one. The teachers' representatives raised numerous objections to the originally proposed method of spending Coronation Day,but submitted anl alternative recommendation that the children should be entertained in four or more separate centres. This, however, the Corona- tion Committee would not agree to and there- fore the whole scheme,as far as the children are concerned—with the exception of the distribution of souvenirs which will take place in the schools on the Wednesday—has 'been cancelled. We do not think its collapse will grieve many people, for two huge school teats on two successive days are oo large an order and one would almost certainly have suffered if the original .intention had been adhered to.

Our encomiums on the THE HEALTH Health Committee do Com/Art-mg AND not seem to have been ITS WORK appreciated for the simple reason that we prefaced them by the remark that the Com- mittee had "shown increased activity in recent years under the spur of an awakening public opinion and an energetic medical officer." The " spur " seems to have pricked the chairman of the com- mittee pretty badly judging by the way he referred to our kindly criticism in the Council meeting.. Alderman James Evans ought to have realised ere now that good work and good deeds thrive on criticism, and bring their own reward. In the past—and a not very far distant past— his Committee was open to the charge of being behindhand in matters effecting public health, and there can be no question that the application of the spur has been beneficial. Even to-day,Alderman Bennett compliments the Committee on having "awakened to the fact that many of the evils were remediable," which would suggest that the members had at one time been asleep. We never went quite so far as this, but we are still of opinion that it is only of late years that the Com- mittee has tackled at all effectively the desperately serious problems of infantile mortality and the housing of the poor. The improvement is striking and obvious, and merits the compliments which the chairman will persist in considering back-handed. His perverted appreciation will not deter us from expressing from time to time a candid opinion, whether favourable or unfavourable, on the important work of the Health Committee.

The proposed new road THE BRIDGE AT bridge at Sankey SANKEY. Bridges is becoming a question of first import- ance. There is neither rhyme nor reason in the stubborn intention of the Lancashire County Council to construct a forty-feet bridge which will not be in the centre of the road, and in face of strong representations from the Corporation of Warrington, when no lees than 80 feet is to be the width of the new Warrington Bridge. Both are on main high roads, and should have a width com- portable with the traffic of to-day and the probable needs cif the future. It is Warring- ton's aim to make Liverpool-road 60 feet wide, but our efforts and expenditure will be largely discounted if the County Council sto have its way. The deputation from Warrington met with scant courtesy and it was apparent that their plea fell on deaf ears. ,1 Alderman Bennett charges the County I Council with thinking not "in continents" but 'in bottle necks" and stigmatises their attitude as "a profound discouragement tol every progressive municipal administrator in the country." The discussion on Tuesday sho wed the local Council to be unanimous in a conviction that the matter should not be allowed to rest where it is. The help of the Local Government Board might very well be invoked to bring the County Council to a more reasonable view of the needs of the moment and the demands of posterity.

It may well be, as THE GIFT OF Mr. Churchill said

SuNtlotrr. pleasantly in the peroration to his vigor- ous and enthusiastic speech at the Guildhall in favour of the Daylight Saving Bill on Wednesday, that future generations ta a brighter and healthier world will "raise statues to Mr. Willett and decorate them with sunflowers on the longest day in the year." But we find it difficult to believe that a reform which has already secured the definite support of "46 chambers of commerce, 170 corporations and town councils, 265 members of Parliament, and 39 trade unions "—not to speak of the 338 ' societies, clubs, and associations which have endorsed it—can be long delayed in our own day and generation. 'The opposition to it has fallen back now upon the forlorn plea— exposed with great thoroughness by the Home Secretary—that everyone can get up an hoiir earlier if he wants to. The answer is simply that everyone can do no such thing. To control custom in the interests of the

worker is the aim of Mr. Willett's great reform: and it is a great reform—far greater than is even yet quite understood. To a generation in which phthisis is increasing at a positively appalling rate, more sun and more air are gifts beyond all prim— " Morning Leader."

Mr. Asquith made an THE TORY " J.P." important statement in MONOPOLY. the House of Commons

on Monday with regard to the method of appointing Justices of the Peace. That statement made it quite plain that the appointments in the Isle of Ely, which had created so much dissatisfaction, were the last which would be made without consulting the Advisory Committees set up in consequence of the Royal Commission Report. These Committees have not been working long enough for their effectiveness to be properly tested. Time is required for the experiment to justify, or fail to justify, itself. It is, of course, absurd to suggest, as the Tory journals do, that Liberals want to be allowed to pack the benches with justices of their own political creed. But when it is frequently asserted by Tory politicians and journals that there is an inherent virtue in Conservative views which specially qualifies their holder for the Justices' bench, it is evident that the gross inequality between Liberals and Conserva- tives which prevails on the magisterial bench cannot be set right without strong measures. Liberals do not claim for a moment that the holding of Liberal opinions is a prima facie recommendation for a man to be made a J.P. It needs a Tory atmosphere to nourish this kind of arrogant assumption. But there is obviously something wrong with a system which gives to the Tory Party, whether in office or in opposition, an overwhelming pre- ponderance on the magisterial bench

_ There were several references to a new disease—tubercularosis—at the meeting of Town Council on Tuesday evening.

To-day is a sad anniversary, for it is just twelve months since the world was pro- foundly shocked by the news that King Edward the Peacemaker had passed away. • _ •

The dear old " Daily Mail," having worked out the health loaf and the thousand-pound sweet-pea, now recommends goat farming as a sure fortune winner. There will be many a butt before success is realised.

The Band of HOpe 'Union never did a happier thing than when it hit upon an an- nual May Festival as a means of advancing the temperance cause. The beautiful spectacle at the Parr Hall on Wednesday and Thursday evenings not only afforded delight to the large audiences but it must have brought home to young and old alike—without seem- to preach at them the indisputable value of the principles for which the organization stands. May the Band of Hope Union long prosper to celebrate the incoming of May in such a charming yet practical fashion !

The Parr Hall organ has deservedly come in for some hard knocks of late, and the local humorist who pennill the topical verses of a song at the Band of Hope festival indulged his sarcasm at the expense of that time- worn and unhonoured instrument. It would be a fine thing if Coronation year could be marked by the installation of an organ worthy of the largest public hall in a town of Warrington's importance. Failing private munificence, the present is hardly the most opportune time to saddle the heavily burdened ratepayers with the expense. Will some wealthy townsman rise to the occasion?

Whictt is the Warrington firm which has booked so many orders for Coronation mugs 'made in Germany?' From first one district, and then another, comes a vehement protest against the contemplated disloyalty of spend- ing money abroad, and apparently the firm in question will reap a nice little harvest from compensation for broken contracts alone. We notice that at Winwick, one parish Councillor was bold enough to advocate the claims of the German mug, but his fellow- members shirked the responsibility of pre- senting to the public a portrait of the English King on a foreign pot. Sentiment is proving ' to be stronger than business, in this instance at least.

Many people who traverse Bridge-street daily will probably be surprised to read the Mayor's description of it as "a whited sepulchre—a veneer of grandeur covering over a mass of squalor:' It is strange but true that many of the fine shops of this modernised thoroughfare back right on to some of the worst hovels and congested areas ,of dwellings in the town. It will be a long time before even the busy centre of Warring- ton is freed from the grimy heritages of those dark days when bye-laws merely regulated public conduct and took no concern of public health.

The Mayor takes a deep interest in the housing question, and at the meeting of the Council on Tuesday he advanced the , important suggestion that as a commemora- tion of the Coronation the town should make a start in the way of providing suitable homes for poor people by erecting an experi- mental block of tenements. London and , Liverpool have done big things on similar lines, and there are plenty of people in Warrington, to whom 3s. a week is a maxi- mum rent, to make the experiment worth a trial. One of the difficulties which besets the Health Committee in demolishing slum property is the re-housing of the dispossessed tenants. We hope the Mayor's suggestion will lead to a practical consideratit n of the question. - -

Mr. Lloyd-George's great scheme of national insurance covering sickness and unemployment is, as "F. C. 0." has aptly remarked, the Result of Employment During Invalidity. In its breadth and importance the proposal is the biggest step in the direction of social experiment which thts generation has ever seen, and at present one finds it impossible to adequately survey the scheme, and forecast the results on poverty, thrift, and the general welfare of the masses of the people. The Chancellor's latest venture,indubitably,involves an epoch- making change in the conditions of living land working, and to say the least it testifies once more to foresight, courage, and con- structive statesmanship of the "uncrowned King of Wales." • •

To many of those who will gather next Tuesday at the Colonial missionary meeting in the Mansion House, it will, the "Christian World" thinks, be interesting to be reminded of the fact that it was built by the Corpora- tion of London with,fmes, which in six years amounted to £15,000, exacted from Noncon- formist citizens, who refused to receive the Sacrament of the Lords Supper according to the rites of the Established Church, and so qualify for the office of Sheriff. Three thus fined appealed, and the case was thirteen

years in reaching the House of Lords, by which time only one, a Mr. Evans, remained alive. Six out of seven judges gave judg- ment in his favour (February 4th, 1767). On that occasion, Mansfield, the Lord Chief Justice, delivered, with what Dr. R. W. Dale described as " noble eloquence," a vindication of the principles of religious justice. _ _

The Opposition in the final debate on Clause 2 of the Parliament Bill, did their best to make our flesh creep as to what is to happen in the Veto-less future and Mr. Kebty-Fletcher indulged in quite a horrible forecast: One of the first things the Government wr,uld he able to do if this elause became law would be to extend their time as a Government for ancther fifteen yews That would give en. the, £75,000 in seamy to the Chancellor of the Exchequer and the Home Secretary, but it would be a very serious thing for the country. . . . . .

If the Memger for Altriacham has " great expectations" this must be an awful prospect for him.

Young Artist's Death. . Students at the Warrington School of Art have heard with deep regret of the death of Mr. Dobson Thomas Clark,who pas,ed away at the early age of 23 rears, at the house of his father, Mr. Arthur Clark,3o, lldarket-streer, Earlestown. Mr. Clark, who had been I practically bedfast for ten months, had great artistic talents and studied for eight years at the Warrington School of Art. He was afterward. • teacher there, and held the full art class teacher's certificate, which cover, many subjects. He also gained a prize f•n- modelling and designing submitted in the national competition. ATTEMPTED RESCUE BY A DOG.

Stanley Yates, of Warrington, and the dog Bogie. Little Yates, who is four, with another child named Pollard, fell into Sankey brook. The dog, which belongs to Pollard's father, and which was with them, first jumped in and tried to Flit Pollard out, and failing, then rushed off and fetched a man named George Holroyd, who rescued Yates. The dog in the meantime ran to Pollard's home and led the boy's sister to the brook by her pinaforet,The child, however, was drowned.

SORE QUEER DISHES.

In some parts of the world reople eat seaweed ; in others, birds' nests ale a delicacy. Hooters of big game enjoy a meal of elephant's foot. Ordinary peotle, however, content themselves with a rational mixed diet; and they doso knowingly or unknowingly in order that they may get all the element, necetwary to the maintesanee of the body in perfect health and I vigour. Some foods supply energy, some make good the daily waste of the system, while others again are merely heat-producing. But there ale a few food, which supply all thrac elements, and of these perhaps the best known and most valuable are currants. These deliojous little fruits (really dried grapes) embody all the nutritive health-promoting properties. That is why they are an etrongly rerommendt d •14 an article of diet all the year rsund. Whatever may be lacking in the °Ater ern stituents of a meal, Currants in some form can always be relied upon to supply the deficiency. Every grocer in the kingdom can supply his customers free with a little recipe book showing the niany simple yet delightful wa's in which Currant. can be prepared for the table.

LAUNCH OF THE "CONQUEROR."

On the Clyde tbis week Britain launched her twentieth Dreadneught. The event was marked by •ge 1 holiday, and huge crowds assembled on the banks of the river. With the launch of the " Concueror " all the eight armoured vessels of the 1909-10 programme are in the water. The vessel, which was named by Lady Pentland, wife of the Secretary for Scotland, is the largest and most formidable battleship ever built en the Clyde. She is $45 feet long, displaces 22,5 f 0 tons, and will develop a speed of twenty-one knots. Her main guns are ten of 13.5, ten broadside, four ahead, four astern guns, and sinless 4-inch enti-torpcdo guns. As a fighting unit the "Conqueror" is superior to any foreign kattleship in a similarly advanced stage of construction.

ANTIQUARIAN CHAPTERL,i

QUEEN ELIZABETH S GLOVES AND

An Hislorical..Gossip With Some

Possible Morals

There probably never lived a vainer woman thm "Good Queen Bess " of England, and if anything were needed to prove that Kings and Queens are only human beings it would be the story of Elizabeth and her fineries. Of her adornments she never tired. What- ever the demands might be upon her time, the was as greatly concerned about her rersonal appear.mee as if she had not had another interest in the world. She craved admiration like a schoolgirl, and had a dis- like to sce any one look better than herself, or dress in a way to attract attention from her own display. One day, as Mrs. Strick- land relates, Lady Mary Howard, one of her maids of honour, appeared in a magnificent velvet dress with a rich border decorated in gold and pearls. Lady Mary was a young and beautiful woman,and showed off her fine gown very well. The rebuke she got from the Queen was Bo severe that the poor maiden laid up her rich vestment and never ventured to wear it again during Elizabeth's life.

These little vanities of the "virgin queen" are as much matters of history, perhaps, as the great transactions of her reign. At any rate, it is safe to say that they were quite as important to her. Her mind was continually occupied by her jewels and dresses, her hair and her hands, and she never forgot, even on the most solemn occasions, that she was a well-dressed woman. She had a hundred

pretty tricks for showing off her charms, and sometimes was quite ridiculous in her attempts to do so. On one occasion, while giving audience in state to the ambassador from the Netherlands, she pulled off and put on her gloves more than a hundred times to display her hands, her splendid rings, and the gloves themselves, which were an elegant and costly pair. Strange to say, her love for finery grew with her years, and in her old age she even increased the number of her decorations, and dressed in a far more elaborate style than in the meridian of her life, foolishly thinking, no doubt, that people would be diverted by these externals from noticing the decay of her personal attractions. Henzmer, a . . .

German traveller who visited England in 1598, when Elizabeth was sixty-six years old, has left us a description of the queen's appearance, which is alike curious and inOresting. _

In person she is very majestic ; her face oblong, fair, but wrinkled ; her eyes small, yet black and pleasant ; a nose a little hooked ; her lips narrow, and her teeth black (a defect the English seem subject to, from their excessive we of sugar). She had in her ears two pearls, with very rich drops; she wore false hair, and that red; upon her head she had a small crown, reported to be made of some of the gold of the celebrated Lunebourg table. Her bosom was uncovered, as all the English ladies have it till they marry, and she had on a necklace of very fine jewels. Her hands were small, her fingers long, and her stature neither tall nor short. Her air was stately, her manner of speaking mild and obliging. That day she was dressed in white silk, bordered with pearls of the size of beans, and over it a mantle ot black silk, shot with silver threads; her train was very long, the end of it borne by a marchioness. Instead of a chain, she had a collar of gold and jewels."

===== 0002601_19110506_art0088.txt | included=true | included =====
THE ADVENT OF GLOVES.

Elizabeth was the first English queen who wore gloves. As articles of a woman's dress they were not used previous to her reign, . .

though gauntlets for men, and long mittens carried tucked in the belt, had been worn a long time by women. For many years gloves were scarce and expensive. Queen Mary Stuart brought from France six pairs of Guernsey worsted gloves, while she had, at the same time, silk stockings interwoven with gold and silver thread, and thirty-six pairs of velvet shoes laced with gold. The gloves worn by Queen Elizabeth and her ladies were very elegant and sumptuous articles. They were lined with white velvet, fastened with gold buttons, wrought with gold, and edged with fringe. These embroidered and trimmed gloves were fashionable a long time (until Queen Anne's reign, I believe.) The University of Cambridge at one time presented the queen with a highly orna- mental pair of glove, coetfng sixty shillings,

An ancient chronicler informs us that her

Majesty, "beholding the beauty of the said gloves, as in great admiration, and in token of her thankful acceptance of the same, held up one of her hands, and smelling into them put them half-way upon her fingers." Perfumed gloves became fashionable towards the middle of her reign. It is said that Edward de Vere, Earl of Oxford, was the first who introduced them into England. This nobleman, on returning from Italy, presented his sovereign a pair of scented gloves, ornamented with roses in colored silks, which pleased her so much that she had a special picture painted which showed her adorned in them.

, Elizabeth was a devoted patronizer of her glovemaker. She put on a pair when her toilet was made in the morning, and she wore them till she disrobed at night. They were of all kinds and prices. One pair was of fine white kid, reaching to the elbows, where they were trimmed with satin and fringed with gold. They were buttoned with jewels, the backs were embroidered with gold bullion and trimmed with gold lace and in the palm of each w-re five little air-holes of the size and shape of melon seeds. ELIZABETH'S RINGS.

Some of these gloves have been preserved, and, though very shapely, they were quite large ;in fact, large enough for a man. This is easily accounted for by the fact that, though her hands were small, her fingers were always covered with rings, several of them of large size. This royal coquette had all the fondness of an Oriental woman for 'jewels, and many of her rings were of great value. Two or three of them possess historical interest. There was her corona- tion ring, which she wore constantly from the time it was put on at her inauguration, never taking it off once for nearly forty-five years. At the end of that time it had become so imbedded in the flesh, and caused so much pain, that she was obliged to have it filed apart, a circumstance that caused her a great deal of concern. When ill-health siezed her, as it did soon afterwards, she blamed the severance of the ring. THE "EARL ESSEX" RING.

In this superstition she will get little sympathy from this generation. There was, however, a ring which brought grief and I possibly the shortening of her days. This was the famous sardony x ring with the cameo head of the queen, which she gave to her favourite, Earl Essex. We all know the

story of the handsome young nobleman,who, gifted with marvellous grace of mind and person, fell at last a victim to the intrigues of his enemies, his own indiscretion, and the jealous petulance of his sovereign. He was her relative, a sort of second cousin, and she loved him in her way, capricious always; one day haughty and distant, the next, when he was ill, condescending enough to goin person to see him and give him broth with her own hands.

When he was quite a youth she had given him the ring promising him that if the time ever came when she should turn against him, even at the last moment, if she received the ring back from him, she would forgive and restore.

There came a day when he committed a political offence that sent him a prisoner to the Tower, where he was condemned to die. The death- warrant was brought for Elizabeth to sign. She did sign it. Then, at the thought of that handsome face being marred by the axe she countermanded it. Finally she signed it again, angry and impatient because the earl failed to send the ring. The ring did not come, and the gay young noble—he was scarcely thirty-four—the k nigh tl lest and the handsomest of all her cavaliers, was led to his doom.

Sho thought 'he had not sent the ring, but he had. Life was sweet to him, as it is to every man who has health and happiness, and lie had dropped the precious ring from his prison window to a boy whom he bade bear it to Lady &rope, his cousin. The lad made a mistake and carried it instead to that lady's sister, the Countess of Nottingham, whose husband was poor Essex's deadly foe. She gave it to the cruel Earl who retained it so that, while the miserable Queen was pacing the floor, looking till the last moment for the token that was to earn her forgiveness, it lay in the keeping of his wicked rival. Later she learned the story from the lips of the dying Countess,but it was too late; the bright young head had fallen, and from that moment she never knew a happy moment.

The Countess was dying when she confessed to the Queen, but it is said that Elizabeth seized her and shook her frail body in a frenzy of rage.

THE RING OF MARY OF SCOTS.

Another ring of Elizabeth that would inspire awe if we could see it to-day was the one given to her by her royal victim Mary of Scots. When Mary Stuart was in prison she sent a valuable diamond ring to her kialous kinswoman. Elizabeth wore it. and with the ring on her finger signed the death warrant of the giver. Strange to say she wore it to the day of her death. Curious thoughts it must have stirred! THE "BLUE RING."

It was an appropriate thing that a ring should he associated with Elizabeth's death, She herself had used them as symbols and now the son of the murdered Mary Stuart was sending one to London to he used as a message and a token. It was a sapphire afterwards known in , court tradition as the 'blue ring," and had been confided to Lady &rope by King James of Scotland, to be forwarded as a token to announce the death of the Queen. All the time the miserable and forlorn queen lay dying, this spy of the Stuart bent over her, watching with lynx-like eyes every breath and motion of England's proud sovereign. When she had sighed her last, Lady Scrope ran to a window and gave intelligence of the fact by silently dropping the blue ring to her brother, Sir Robert Carey, who was lurking beneath. He galloped with all speed, taking a fresh horse at every stopping place to bear the token to James at Edinburgh. Carey reached Holy- rood the following night just after the king, had retired. The "blue ring" was handed to him in bed. He arose, called for his clothes and started at once for London to claim the crown, which a few days after- wards was put upon his head. A.M

FROM LABOUR'S STANDPOINT.

By Philip Snowden, M.P.

VOTES FOR WOMEN—AND FOR MEN. The discussion of Votes for Women is now again to the fore. The success of Sir George Kemp in the ballot for private members' has given the opportunity for a Second Read- ing debate this week in the House of Commons on this subject. The great debate which occupied two days last session, and which ended in a great majority for the !Second Reading of what was known as the ;Conciliation Bill, gave a great impetus to the movement for the enfranchisement of women. At the time of writing the fate of the new Bill on Second Reading is not known, but it is safe to predict that it will be passed by the new Parliament by as large a majority ,as the last Parliament gave a Second Read- ing to a somewhat similar Bill.

The Bill which will be debated on Friday has been framed with the object of securing the largest amount of support for some measure which will remove the sex disquali- fication from the Parliamentary franchise. The difficulties in the way of securing any- thing like united action among the various sections who are in favour of some form of woman franchise have been very great. It has been a matter of reconciling extremes. At one end there has been the advanced democrat who favours adult suffrage and the removal of all franchise qualifications based on any form of property. At the other end there has been the moderate man,who thinks that the possession of property or the pay- ment of rates and taxes should en title a woman to vote, just as these qualifications now I entitle a man to vote. There are Radicals who 'support Votes for Women; there are Unionists who do the same. There are Radicals who are opposed to Votes for Women; there are Unionists who join with the Radicals in this opposition. There is no party majority in the House of Commons in favour of Votes for Women. About ono- third of the Liberal members will vote against any Bill which proposes to enfranchise women. A little less than one- half of the Unionist party is divided upon the question, too. If either Liberal or Unionist party were to make this a party question it could not carry the Bill, whatever form the Bill were to take, whether it proposed full adult suffrag or it was limited to giving the vote to ratepaying women. THE ONLY WAY.

There is only one way of settling this question for the time being, and that is by compromise which will unite, in sufficient numbers, the members of all parties who realise the position, and are willing to sink personal views and agree upon the Bill which will command the largest measure of support. The debate on last year's Bill served to indicate the lines which such a Bill should follow. The Bill which is now before Parlia- ment has been amended in some respects so as to meet objections which were put forward last year. The Bill as it now stands proposes to 'extend the .'arliamentary franchise to women householders. It does not permit the duplication of votes under the £lO occupation franchise which applies to the municipal franchise. It does not permit a husband and wife to be both registered as voters in the same constituency. It was urged against the Bill of last year that it would permit the duplication of votes. There never was much foundation for that fear, but the Conciliation Committee have been most anxious to remove every ground of objection against the Bill. . .

The Bill now proposes to extend the Parliamentary vote to women who are qualified to be registered as municipal voters. It would give the Parliamentary vote to about one million women in the United Kingdom. It will give the vote to the women who by general consent are most entitled to it, namely the women who pay rates and taxes and have the responsibilities of keeping a home and household together. Marriage is not a disqualification. It is true that only a comparatively few married women will qualify, because as a rule married women do not stand in the position of house- holders. But there are quite a number of instances where the house is in the name of the wife, and in such cases the wife will get the vote under the Bill. In Dundee no less than nine per cent, of the women who will get the Parliamentary vote under this Bill will be married women.

UNREASONABLE CRITICISM

The Bill will be misrepresented again as a

Bill to give Votes to Ladies, and as—being so devised as to exclude working class women. It is difficult to deal with a persistent mis- representation of this sort with patience, Lecause those who make it must be either unprincipled or unpardonably ignorant of the character of the Bill. The Bill will give the Parliamentary vote to women of whom ninety per cent, will belong to the working class. The facts in support of this statement have been put forward so often that one is tired of repeating them, but in view of the continued misrepresentation of the effect of the Bill it is necessary to give the great facts again. The most careful investigations have been made in different parts of the country, and the results confirm what everybody with knowledge knew to be the fact, namely that nine-tenths of the municipal voters are women of the working class. A census was recently taken in Dundee as a typical town. The result was as follows :—Women of independent means 7'5 per cent., professional and business women 31 per cent., wage earning women 4313, working class house wives 35.3 per cent. There is a great amount of support in the country for such a Bill as this. About fifty Town Councils have passed resolutions in support of the Bill. A Bill of this sort is the only measure of woman franchise which is likely to pass this Parliament. As has been pointed out there is not a majority in favour of a wider measure of enfranchisement. An Adult Suffrage Bill would not get one-third of the votes of the present House of Commons. Many strong opponents of women suffrage openly confess they will try to amend this Bill in Committee so as to give Adult Suffrage, knowing that by that means they can certainly kill it on Third Reading. TIME FOR A CONCESSION.

It is really time that this disturbing ques- tion was taken out of the political battlefield. The women have fought for the vote long and valiantly. This subject has been debated in the House of Commons no less than twenty times. Bills for the enfranchisement of women have passed Second Reading six times. In every Parliament for the last twenty-five years there has been a majority

in favour of -votes for women. No move- ment for reform has shown more activity in recent years than this. The most imposing political demonstrations of this generation have been organised by the women suffra-

gists. Over 5,000 meetings have been held in support of the Conciliation Bill, including a demonstration in Hyde Park which,

'according to the newspapers, was larger than the great gatherings there forty years ago when the men were agitating for household franchise!

PROGRESS ABROAD.

While the suffragists in this country have been agitating for the vote, the cause has been making great practical headway in other lands. Five of the American States have given the vote to women, and in others the Bill to grant it has passed the State Legislature and is only waiting the referendum of the people. The women have the vote throughout the Australian common- wealth, and in New Zealand. Finland has woman suffrage and had a large number of women in its Diet. Norway gave woman suffrage in 1908. The success of these experiments is undoubted. Last November the Australian Senate passed unanimously a resolution declaring that the extension of the Suffrage to women on the same terms as men has had most beneficial results. It was decided to cable this opinion to the Prime Minister of Great Britair.. The House of Representatives has since passed a similar resolution.

- - The passing of such a measure of enfran- chisement as this Bill proposes will in no way prejudice any future extension of the franchise. What it will do will be to assure that any extension in the future will apply to women and men equally. If there be an extension of the franchise to men before women have first got the sex barrier removed it is more than probable that women will be excluded. The Bill is one which is much more moderate than most democrats would desire, but it is the best which can be got in the present state of Parliamentary opinion on the subject, and it is on. that ought to be passed in the present session of Parliament.

CHILDREN'S CDRNER, Conduct(d by UrCIE CALEB _

THE OLDEST TRAVEL STORY IN OUR

LANGUAGE

Not long ago I was reading what is called the " Anglo Saxon Chronicle," a fine old record written to tell us of the doings of King Alfred the Oreat and the Saxon Kings who followed him. By chance I saw a brief narrative which tells how King Alfred sent a whale fisher named Othere upon a voyage of exploration. He was to go to the land we call Scandinavia, and bring word what kind of people were living there. We should like the story to be longer because it is very good. It is chiefly interesting because it is the earliest travel story in our language. It is much more than a thousand years old.

THE VOYAGE Or OTHiftF,.

()there told his lord, King Alfred, that he had dwelt in a place farther north than any Northmen had ever lived—by the shore of the Western Sea. It was nothing but desert land in that part of the world, except where a few Finns, who hunted in winter and fished in summer, had their huts. He had sailed along the coast of this waste land farther north than any whale fisher had ever done, and had sailed inland up a great river, but not for a great distance,for fear of unfriendly inhabitants.

lie visited this wild North Land for the sake of hunting the walrus,or " horse-whale" as he called it, which has very long teeth, some of which he brought home, and showed the King,

The skin of the walrus was used for ship- ropes, and in size it was said to be much smaller than the whale.

In that land a man was said to be rich if he owned many reindeer. At the time .hat Othere sought out the King, he was the owner of six hundred tame deer, and among them were six "decoy-deer,- who were very precious, since they were used to catch their companions in the wild, and bring them in to be tamed.

He told the King that he was one of the richest men in the land, for he owned more than twenty oxen and twenty sheep and twenty pigs; and if he wanted to plough he ploughed with horses.

Money and taxes were paid in that land by means of deer skins, birds feathers, walrus bone, ship ropes, according to his rank. The highest in the land had to pay as a tax fifteen skins of martens, five of reindeer, one bear skin, ten measures of feathers, a cloak

of bear skin or other skin, and two chip ropes. , . . . .

, He said also that the land of the Northman was very long and narrow. The only part on which man could plough or feed his cattle lay along the coast-line, and even that was very rocky in places. Wild moorlands lay to the east, facing the cultivated land, and in these parts dwelt Finns, who were for ever ravaging the fields which the Northman had tilled, SO that there was constant warfare between the two races.

Aid then mamma came down the staire, And swung the pantry door. And there it hung upon its jag As it bad hung before. In low and rigid tones Gretchen Madeline Runes began her story. "My father's es tates adjoined those of Count Saloski. Feli- cia, his daughter, was indeed his only child. We met for the first time at a convent on the borders of the Pyrennces. My parents were wealthy, but having descended from a trad- ing stock, were not considered by the neigh- bouring nobility worthy of their high and mighty society. My tether was of German extraction, and being opposed to autocratic rule, he was consequently worshipped by the monjiks, while Saloski was detected by his dependents. The old man was tyrannical, and the Countess severely austere. Repres- sion and the knout was Saloski's idea of rul- ing the peesants in his service, and his treat- ment added fuel to the blare that W2B 11111- Ding through the country. Felicia was entirely different from her overbearing parents, her heart was of anal- toyed gold. She was tender and sympathetic and the poor people idolised her. There was universal unrest amongst the enlightened classes, and the Cossack butchers quelled re volt after revolt by murdering men, women, and children with equal impartiality. With Merging rapidity the discontent spread and at last reached our isolated home. But our moujiks were loyal, they had been treated properly, and this saved Count Saloski from being knonted by Isis own people. In the midst of the revolt against the iron sway of a few autocrats, Felicia and I left France, our education finished. roe safety we and the Salonki's migrated to Moscow, and it was in that city that Felicia met Prince Sergins Kolakowaki. Their tanned ideals of life be- ing east in the name mould, small wonder that they fell in love with earls other. Prince Kotakowski had travelled all over Europe, and while in England had become a convert :to the idea of a government in which the people should have a share. This, of course, Was rank heresy, and the more he preached the more ho was laughed at. Scoffing and threats were of no avail; a conspiracy grew up against him, and practically his doom was sealed. Rut through the storm the Prince remained steadfast to the cause that he es- poused. The cabal raised against him be- gan to take definite shape when he was the chosen favourite of the Cent. The ellon last Saturday was to help to provide funds for equipment, !sad the tummer camps. PEACE MEETING AT LYMM.

A meeting was held after the ordinary Sunday evenlog service at the Lymm Primitive Methodist School in support of the proposed Arbitration treaty between this oountry and the United States of America. The meting was under the auspices of the L 3 mm Free Church Council. Mr. S. Moss presided. The Rev. A. Barrett said peace was one of the ' greatest insets in the social life of the nation. If there were rival and warring factions, thee progress was hindered. When an element of disturbance crept into: the netional life, roomy materials that were nettled for education and social reform were diverted to manu- facture engines of destruction; men were traced to slay one another, end the Kingdom of God which Christ came to establish was hindered. He did not my that war might never have been necessary ; but when , war had been necesaary men bad gone out often enough in the wrong spirit. It was the state of spirit which men could go but to war which had to be ' considered, and as they knew when men went out to war they went out full of malice against their fellow beings, who were as dear to Clod as they were them- selves. Now, as Christian people be said they were forced to ask themselves : What did Jeans Christ my ? They could not deny OW universal friendship wan one of the principles of the Christian'. life. They who were met there did not believe that Jmus Christ was impractimble, they believed that J.... Christ was speaking words of wisdom, and that He wished them to follow Him. It would be well for them to recognise what war was Ile coiled it " murder" Christ's temhing was perfectly plain ; in regard to war Ile bad come to lead their feet in the way of peace, and it was their duty to establish pence—peace with their neighbours, in social life, and in international relations,--and to pot it open a perinment footing in the world. President Taft said theta treety of arbitration ghoul I be drawn up so that every cause of dispute should be referred to an impar- tial tribunal. That was merely the application to international relations of the same principles they exercised in civil life. Fighting settled nothing. It never decided on which aide justice reigned, and the present suggestion am that as regarded the United States and Britain they should settle their disputee by arbitration. One did not expect in the immediate future that our armaments would cost less, but it tem the begiuning of so end. He saw in one of the paper. that it was stated that the people of the continent were laughing at Us. Well they could afford to let them laugh. It took more courage to take a step sneh u they were taking than to lead an army to the battlefield. It uould be the most glorious day since Jesus Christ was raised from the dead when the treaty was aimed by the two cations who bets.sa them owned more than me-third of the world's surface, ami sled over more than 150 million people. But i. be imagined that there would not aril. Cancer toe G,pote in the future. Throughout the whole of the performance at the Peace and Hippodrome thin week one in bound to thoroughly enjoy oneself, for each item not only tworn the murk of originality but it is presented in an admirable manner. Bromwich and Misses Katheiine Clerks and Ails& Lorne. The main scene is laid in a newepaper office and the story, which is dramatically portrayed, is that of the awful ravages of drink. Mmkinnon's company,provides capital entertainment, their singing being of a high order and their humorous dancing super). They are also at home on various instruments. Mr. Snowden, M.P , and Parliamentary

Conscience.

Sir,—Having mad Mr. P Snowden's article in your issue of last Saturday, on the qmstion of policy, will you allow me to state that so long as many members of Parliament vote against their convictims, the country will often he injured by those who govern it. It means " I i ght for ever on the scaffold and wrong for ever on the throre." If members of Parliament were to go to the House of Commons for selfish pur- poses, and to disregard the welfare of the country, then they might he actiog consistently in voting against their convictions. But if their only object is to promote the welfare of the country. I cannot see how men can reasonably v, to against what they believe would be beneficial, and for what they believe would be injutioua If tneuibers of Parliament were to vote with the Government for what they believe to be injurious,and tithe Government in return were to pass measures which in reality they did not approve, what would become of poor old England ? Would it not be a case of "weep I.r Albion weep" If a Government that (lid not believe in Home Rule were to pase a llotne Rule Bill in-order to obtain the Irish vote ; if a Government that did not believe in members of Parliament being raid by the State, or in reversing the Oaliorne judgment, were to pars a Bill for the payment of members awl also to river-to the Osborne Judgment, in older to obtain the labour vote, what a deplorable condition of things there would be. Of coarse it is different when the Government's only object is the nation's welfare, but the methods I have mentioned ought never to be adopted by any government or party or men. Though I think Mr. Snowden very worthy of respect, I think he is mistaken in the view which he takes of this subject. Political honesty is a duty which public men owe to God and mankind for it is only by truthfulness and justice that the world can be improved, and these ought never to lie violated for any government or party or cause. Without being very good, if we are not very bad, we ought not to be indifferent to the welfare of humanity or to disregard cur dim anticipations of a better tirne.—Yours, S. KENYON. Ribbleton, Ility I.lro, 1911.

I G TAILOR

VIXI.

The Rev. A. I. Mitchell and the

Trade.

Sir,—l see by your valued issue of to-day's date that at the meeting of the Warrinaton Guardians, the Rev. A. M. Mitchell opposed the election of another gentleman to office becau-e lie derives some p rtion of his income from the to ore the rev. gentleman's vrords, drink traffic." I have not the pleasure of knowing the gentleman objected to. but I gather that in the eyes of the objector, he is well fitted, personally, for the office to which his fellow members have elected him and that his only, presumed, disqualification is his business, or part of his business. This being so may I ask you to allow toe a small portion of your space for a protest against an attack no lacking in toleration? Because a gentleman is connected with the sale of drink is he any less able and willing to work for the good government of hill town, or is he less endowed with clpacity to fairly administer the law of the land than an. ther trio obtains his dads bread by carrying our the duties of a clerg3 Stan? Surely no fair minded man can believe so firs moment. Yet this is what the Rev. A. M. Mitchell would seem to suggest in his spi7ch. . . .

From anwher column of your iesue I gather that the idea underlying the objection is that this gentle- man might lend a friendly ear,on the Assessment Com- mittee, t•i applications from brewere and publicans for reduced asseastnents ass result of the" People's Budget,' whilst another might refuse such reductions. If this is so, then it may be said with truth that the law, which has decided that such reductions moat be made, would be loss likely tl be fairly administered by a member of the views of the Rev. A. M. Mitchell ; with the result that the ratepayers would not ouly have to bear the additional burdens placed upon them, by a budget nlnth was being advertised as being for their special benefit, but the cost of expensive litigation as well. The members of the Board may, therefore, be congratulated upon an election which, in the long run, will be for the greater advantage of the public as a whole.-. Yours, etc., 29th April, 1911.

A. HAND.

WARRINGTON MINISTERS CLAIM

Assize Action Settled

An action to recover money lent war brought before Mr. Juetice Banker and • special jury at kl•nchester Aeriser, yesterday, by the Rev. Joseph Howard, of Sankey Hall, hßarrington. against William Boles, hawker, of Latchford. Mr. Taylor, N.C., and Dr. Atkinson appear. d for the plaintiff, and Mr. Acton for the .defendant.

Mr. Taylor, in explaining the case to the jury, sMd that .CII2, the sum claimed, bad less lent at various times in 1909 by the plaintiff to the defendant to enable , him to start furnishing removal businer in Birming• ham at premises belonging to the former, and upon which he spent a considerable sum in alterations for the purpose. No stipulation was made at the time alto rent or interest, the undemanding being that the question should be left over foragastiver months in order to see how the business went on and that they thee should comatose agreement with regard to it. The defendant was not at Birmingham more than two or three months when, bis wife tiring of her life there, he gave the business up and retnrned to Warrington. In the following January the plaintiff asked the defendant what hew.. going to do towards repaying the borrowed money. and the defendant at first asked for time. This was given him, Init in May he del ined to pay, alleging that the plaintiff bed bought a picture from him which had turned out to be valuable, and that Mr. Howard should be content with that.

-- The defence was a denial that the money was lent at all, and that if it was, it was on terms of mutual partnership. This the plaintiff denied. After evidence by the plaintiff to this effect the panics Came to terms, and a verdict wae given by con. aunt for the plaintiff for £lOO, with stay of execution for sir months.

In accordance with the terms of the agreement Mr. Acton unreservedly withdrew all allegation of partner- ship beta een the plaintiff and defendant, and admitted that the latter had been entirely under a misapprehen- sion on tlie point. Ile also withdrew the allegations as to the picture ant as to any remuneration being due to the defendant as manager of the Birmingham business.

Judgment waa given in accordance with the verdict.
